<a id='question-148'></a>

### Question 148: Gallbladder and Biliary Tract Pathology
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of the epidemiology, clinical presentation, and histopathology of gallbladder cancer, specifically its association with chronic cholelithiasis and porcelain gallbladder, and its increased incidence in certain populations like Native Americans. (本题考察胆囊癌的流行病学、临床表现和组织病理学，特别是其与慢性胆石症和瓷器胆囊的关系，以及其在特定人群如美洲原住民中的发病率增加。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 68-year-old Navajo woman presents to the emergency department with a 3-month history of worsening right upper quadrant abdominal pain, anorexia, and unintentional weight loss of 15 lbs. She has a known history of symptomatic cholelithiasis for 20 years, managed conservatively. Her past medical history is significant for type 2 diabetes mellitus and hypertension. Physical examination reveals mild jaundice, scleral icterus, and a palpable, non-tender gallbladder mass in the right upper quadrant. Laboratory tests show elevated total bilirubin (4.5 mg/dL, normal <1.2 mg/dL), alkaline phosphatase (450 U/L, normal 40-120 U/L), and AST/ALT (180 U/L/150 U/L, normal <40 U/L). An abdominal ultrasound reveals a thickened gallbladder wall (1.2 cm), multiple gallstones, and a heterogeneous mass within the gallbladder lumen measuring 5 cm. A CT scan confirms the gallbladder mass and shows no evidence of distant metastasis. A cholecystectomy is performed. Gross examination of the resected gallbladder reveals a large, ulcerated, infiltrative mass replacing most of the gallbladder wall. Microscopic examination shows moderately differentiated adenocarcinoma with invasion through the muscularis propria into the perimuscular connective tissue. Immunohistochemical staining is positive for CK7 and CK19, and negative for CK20 and CDX2.

What is the most likely diagnosis?

(A) Cholangiocarcinoma arising from the cystic duct
(B) Metastatic adenocarcinoma from the colon
(C) Gallbladder adenocarcinoma
(D) Hepatocellular carcinoma with biliary invasion
(E) Adenomyomatosis of the gallbladder

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Demographics & History:** 68-year-old Navajo woman with a 20-year history of symptomatic cholelithiasis. Navajo ethnicity is a known risk factor for gallbladder cancer. Chronic cholelithiasis is the most significant risk factor for gallbladder cancer.
*   **Symptoms:** Worsening RUQ pain, anorexia, weight loss over 3 months. These are classic symptoms suggestive of malignancy, particularly in the context of chronic gallbladder disease.
*   **Physical Exam:** Jaundice, scleral icterus, palpable RUQ mass. Jaundice suggests biliary obstruction, potentially by a tumor. A palpable mass is highly suspicious for malignancy.
*   **Labs:** Elevated bilirubin, alkaline phosphatase, AST/ALT. These findings indicate cholestasis and hepatocellular injury, consistent with biliary obstruction.
*   **Imaging:** Ultrasound shows thickened gallbladder wall, gallstones, and a heterogeneous mass. CT confirms the mass without distant metastasis. These findings strongly suggest a primary gallbladder tumor.
*   **Surgery & Pathology:** Cholecystectomy performed. Gross pathology shows an infiltrative mass replacing the gallbladder wall. Microscopic examination reveals moderately differentiated adenocarcinoma invading through the muscularis propria. This confirms malignancy originating from the gallbladder epithelium.
*   **Immunohistochemistry:** Positive for CK7 and CK19, negative for CK20 and CDX2. This profile is characteristic of biliary origin (gallbladder/bile duct epithelium), distinguishing it from colorectal origin (which would typically be positive for CK20 and CDX2).

All these clues point towards a primary malignancy of the gallbladder, specifically adenocarcinoma, given the microscopic findings and immunohistochemistry.

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
*   **Pathophysiology:** Gallbladder cancer typically arises from the gallbladder epithelium, often in the setting of chronic inflammation caused by gallstones. The chronic irritation and inflammation lead to epithelial hyperplasia, dysplasia, and eventually carcinoma in situ, followed by invasive adenocarcinoma. Porcelain gallbladder (calcification of the gallbladder wall) is also a strong risk factor.
*   **Histopathology:** The most common type is adenocarcinoma (around 85-90%). Histological grading (well, moderately, poorly differentiated) and staging (TNM) are crucial for prognosis and treatment. Invasion through the muscularis propria (as seen here) indicates at least a T2 stage.
*   **Immunohistochemistry:** Biliary epithelial markers like CK7 and CK19 are typically positive in gallbladder cancer. Markers associated with colorectal origin, such as CK20 and CDX2, are usually negative, helping to differentiate it from metastatic colorectal cancer.
*   **Epidemiology:** Gallbladder cancer is relatively rare globally but has a significantly higher incidence in certain populations, including Native Americans (especially Navajo), Hispanics, and Asians. Chronic cholelithiasis is the major risk factor worldwide.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Cholangiocarcinoma arising from the cystic duct:** While cholangiocarcinoma can occur in the biliary tree, including the cystic duct, the pathology description clearly states the tumor originated *within* the gallbladder wall and replaced it. Cystic duct cholangiocarcinoma would typically present as a mass near the cystic duct insertion, potentially causing biliary obstruction, but the primary tumor site described is the gallbladder itself. Immunohistochemistry (CK7+/CK19+/CK20-/CDX2-) is consistent with biliary origin but doesn't specifically pinpoint the cystic duct over the gallbladder. The gross description of the mass replacing the gallbladder wall strongly favors a primary gallbladder tumor.
- (B) **Metastatic adenocarcinoma from the colon:** Metastatic colorectal cancer can spread to the gallbladder, but it is less common than primary gallbladder cancer. The immunohistochemistry profile (CK7+/CK19+/CK20-/CDX2-) argues strongly against colorectal origin. Colorectal adenocarcinoma is typically CK20 positive and CDX2 positive. The clinical presentation (long history of gallstones, palpable gallbladder mass) is also more typical for primary gallbladder cancer.
- (D) **Hepatocellular carcinoma with biliary invasion:** Hepatocellular carcinoma (HCC) originates from hepatocytes in the liver. While HCC can invade the biliary system, it does not typically arise *within* the gallbladder wall itself. The pathology description clearly indicates adenocarcinoma originating from the gallbladder epithelium, not hepatocytes. HCC would show different immunohistochemical markers (e.g., Hep Par 1 positive, CK7/CK19 negative).
- (E) **Adenomyomatosis of the gallbladder:** Adenomyomatosis is a benign condition characterized by hyperplasia of the gallbladder mucosa and muscularis, with invagination of the mucosa into the muscle layer (Rokitansky-Aschoff sinuses). It can cause gallbladder wall thickening and sometimes RUQ pain, but it is not a malignancy. The presence of an infiltrative mass, ulceration, invasion through the muscularis propria, and adenocarcinoma on histology definitively rules out adenomyomatosis.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):

| Feature                 | Gallbladder Adenocarcinoma (Correct) | Cholangiocarcinoma (Cystic Duct) | Metastatic Colorectal Cancer | Hepatocellular Carcinoma | Adenomyomatosis |
| :---------------------- | :---------------------------------- | :------------------------------ | :--------------------------- | :----------------------- | :-------------- |
| **Origin**              | Gallbladder epithelium              | Biliary epithelium (cystic duct) | Colon epithelium            | Liver hepatocytes        | Gallbladder epithelium/muscle |
| **Gross Appearance**    | Infiltrative mass, ulceration       | Mass near cystic duct insertion | Variable, may mimic primary   | Liver mass, invasion     | Wall thickening, sinuses |
| **Histology**           | Adenocarcinoma                      | Adenocarcinoma                  | Adenocarcinoma              | Hepatocellular carcinoma | Hyperplasia      |
| **Invasion**            | Through muscularis propria          | Variable                        | Variable                     | Variable                 | None             |
| **CK7**                 | Positive                            | Positive                        | Negative                     | Negative                 | Variable        |
| **CK19**                | Positive                            | Positive                        | Negative                     | Negative                 | Variable        |
| **CK20**                | Negative                            | Variable                        | Positive                     | Negative                 | Negative        |
| **CDX2**                | Negative                            | Variable                        | Positive                     | Negative                 | Negative        |
| **Risk Factors**        | Gallstones, porcelain gallbladder, Native American ethnicity | Chronic biliary inflammation | Colorectal cancer          | Cirrhosis, HBV/HCV       | Unknown         |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Gallbladder cancer is strongly associated with chronic cholelithiasis and is more common in certain populations like Native Americans. It typically presents as an infiltrative adenocarcinoma. Differentiating it from other biliary malignancies (cholangiocarcinoma) and metastatic disease (especially colorectal) relies on clinical context, imaging, and immunohistochemistry (CK7/CK19 positive, CK20/CDX2 negative for gallbladder/biliary origin). Benign conditions like adenomyomatosis must be excluded based on histology showing malignancy and invasion.

<a id='question-149'></a>

### Question 149: Gallbladder and Biliary Tract Pathology: Focus 03-49 (Pigmented BilirubinateStones)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: The question tests the understanding of the pathogenesis and composition of pigmented gallstones (specifically black pigment stones) and their association with chronic hemolytic conditions, leading to increased unconjugated bilirubin and calcium bilirubinate precipitation. (问题考察的是色素性胆结石（特别是黑色色素性结石）的病理发生机制和组成，以及它们与慢性溶血性疾病的关系，导致未结合胆红素增加和胆红素钙沉淀。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old African American woman with a known history of sickle cell anemia presents to the emergency department complaining of severe, constant right upper quadrant abdominal pain radiating to her back, nausea, and vomiting for the past 12 hours. She reports similar, milder episodes in the past. Her vital signs show a temperature of 38.5°C (101.3°F), heart rate of 110 bpm, blood pressure of 100/60 mmHg, and respiratory rate of 20 breaths/min. Physical examination reveals marked tenderness to palpation in the right upper quadrant with guarding and a positive Murphy's sign. Laboratory results show elevated total bilirubin (4.5 mg/dL, normal <1.2 mg/dL), elevated alkaline phosphatase (350 U/L, normal 40-120 U/L), elevated AST (180 U/L, normal <40 U/L), and elevated amylase (150 U/L, normal <100 U/L). An abdominal ultrasound reveals a distended gallbladder with wall thickening (>3 mm) and pericholecystic fluid. Multiple echogenic foci with posterior acoustic shadowing are noted within the gallbladder lumen, consistent with gallstones. Given her history and presentation, which of the following is the most likely composition of the gallstones?

(A) Primarily cholesterol crystals with minimal calcium bilirubinate.
(B) Primarily calcium bilirubinate with minimal cholesterol.
(C) Primarily cholesterol crystals with significant calcium bilirubinate and calcium carbonate.
(D) Primarily calcium bilirubinate with significant cholesterol and calcium carbonate.
(E) Primarily calcium bilirubinate with significant cholesterol and calcium phosphate.

#### Correct Answer: (B)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Profile:** 62-year-old African American woman with sickle cell anemia. Sickle cell anemia is a chronic hemolytic condition.
*   **Symptoms:** Severe RUQ pain radiating to the back, nausea, vomiting (classic biliary colic symptoms, suggestive of cholecystitis or choledocholithiasis).
*   **Vital Signs:** Fever (38.5°C), tachycardia (110 bpm), hypotension (100/60 mmHg), tachypnea (20 breaths/min) - suggest systemic inflammation, possibly sepsis secondary to cholecystitis or cholangitis.
*   **Physical Exam:** RUQ tenderness, guarding, positive Murphy's sign - strongly indicative of acute cholecystitis.
*   **Labs:** Elevated total bilirubin, alkaline phosphatase, AST, amylase - suggest biliary obstruction and inflammation, potentially involving the pancreas (gallstone pancreatitis).
*   **Imaging:** Ultrasound shows distended gallbladder, wall thickening, pericholecystic fluid, and gallstones - confirms acute cholecystitis and cholelithiasis.
*   **Question:** Asks for the *most likely composition* of the gallstones in this patient.
*   **Key Link:** The patient has sickle cell anemia, a chronic hemolytic condition. Chronic hemolysis leads to increased breakdown of red blood cells and release of hemoglobin. Hemoglobin is metabolized to unconjugated bilirubin. The liver conjugates bilirubin, but in chronic hemolysis, the amount of unconjugated bilirubin overwhelms the liver's conjugation capacity, leading to increased levels of unconjugated bilirubin in the bile. Unconjugated bilirubin is poorly soluble in aqueous solutions. In the gallbladder, this excess unconjugated bilirubin precipitates with calcium to form calcium bilirubinate, which is the primary component of black pigment stones.

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
*   **Pathogenesis of Pigment Stones:** Pigment stones are primarily composed of calcium bilirubinate. They are further classified into black pigment stones and brown pigment stones.
    *   **Black Pigment Stones:** Form in sterile bile within the gallbladder. They are associated with chronic hemolytic conditions (e.g., sickle cell anemia, hereditary spherocytosis, thalassemia) or cirrhosis. These conditions lead to increased production of unconjugated bilirubin. The excess unconjugated bilirubin precipitates with calcium in the gallbladder to form calcium bilirubinate polymers, which are insoluble and form the nidus of the stone. These stones are typically small, multiple, and brittle.
    *   **Brown Pigment Stones:** Form in infected bile, often within the biliary ducts (choledocholithiasis). They are associated with biliary tract infections (e.g., *E. coli*, *Klebsiella*, *Ascaris lumbricoides*) and biliary stasis. Bacteria produce β-glucuronidase, which deconjugates conjugated bilirubin back to unconjugated bilirubin. This unconjugated bilirubin then precipitates with calcium to form calcium bilirubinate. These stones are typically softer, larger, and may be associated with ductal dilation.
*   **Patient's Condition:** The patient has sickle cell anemia (chronic hemolysis). This leads to increased unconjugated bilirubin. The stones formed in her gallbladder are therefore likely black pigment stones, primarily composed of calcium bilirubinate.
*   **Cholesterol Stones:** These are the most common type of gallstones (80-90% in Western countries). They form due to cholesterol supersaturation in bile, often associated with factors like obesity, female sex, age, rapid weight loss, and certain medications (e.g., estrogen). They are typically composed of cholesterol monohydrate crystals, with varying amounts of calcium bilirubinate, calcium carbonate, and mucin glycoproteins.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Primarily cholesterol crystals with minimal calcium bilirubinate. Incorrect. This describes the composition of typical cholesterol stones, which are not the type primarily associated with chronic hemolysis like sickle cell anemia. The patient's hemolytic condition predisposes her to pigment stones.
- (C): Primarily cholesterol crystals with significant calcium bilirubinate and calcium carbonate. Incorrect. While cholesterol stones contain some calcium bilirubinate and calcium carbonate, they are primarily cholesterol. This option incorrectly identifies cholesterol as the primary component in a patient with chronic hemolysis, where pigment stones (calcium bilirubinate) are expected.
- (D): Primarily calcium bilirubinate with significant cholesterol and calcium carbonate. Incorrect. While pigment stones are primarily calcium bilirubinate, they typically contain *minimal* cholesterol. The presence of significant cholesterol would suggest a mixed stone or primarily a cholesterol stone, which is less likely given the strong association between chronic hemolysis and black pigment stones.
- (E): Primarily calcium bilirubinate with significant cholesterol and calcium phosphate. Incorrect. Similar to (D), this option incorrectly suggests a significant amount of cholesterol in pigment stones. Calcium phosphate is not a major component of either cholesterol or pigment stones.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):
| Feature             | Cholesterol Stones                                  | Black Pigment Stones (Calcium Bilirubinate)                 | Brown Pigment Stones (Calcium Bilirubinate)                 |
| :------------------ | :-------------------------------------------------- | :---------------------------------------------------------- | :---------------------------------------------------------- |
| **Primary Component** | Cholesterol monohydrate crystals                     | Calcium bilirubinate                                         | Calcium bilirubinate                                         |
| **Associated Factors** | Obesity, female sex, age, rapid weight loss, estrogen | Chronic hemolysis (sickle cell, spherocytosis, thalassemia), cirrhosis | Biliary infection, biliary stasis, *Ascaris lumbricoides*     |
| **Bile Environment** | Cholesterol supersaturation                           | Increased unconjugated bilirubin (sterile bile)               | Increased unconjugated bilirubin (infected bile)               |
| **Location**          | Gallbladder (most common)                            | Gallbladder                                                 | Biliary ducts (most common)                                 |
| **Appearance**        | Faceted, yellowish                                   | Small, multiple, brittle, black                             | Soft, greasy, brown                                         |
| **Patient Profile**   | Typical Western patient                               | Patients with chronic hemolysis or cirrhosis                 | Patients with biliary infection or stasis                   |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Chronic hemolytic conditions like sickle cell anemia lead to increased unconjugated bilirubin, which precipitates with calcium in the gallbladder to form black pigment gallstones, primarily composed of calcium bilirubinate. Differentiate pigment stones (associated with hemolysis or infection) from cholesterol stones (associated with metabolic factors). Remember that brown pigment stones are associated with infection, while black pigment stones are associated with sterile hemolysis or cirrhosis.

<a id='question-150'></a>

### Question 150: Gallbladder and Biliary Tract Pathology: Focus 03-50 (These stones are composed of calcium salts and unc)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of the composition and pathogenesis of different types of gallstones, specifically focusing on the distinction between cholesterol stones (most common) and pigment stones (calcium bilirubinate stones), and their association with specific clinical conditions and risk factors. (考察胆石症的类型、组成和发病机制，特别是胆固醇石和色素石（钙盐和未结合胆红素）的区别，以及它们与特定临床状况和风险因素的关系。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old woman with a history of chronic hemolytic anemia due to sickle cell disease presents to the emergency department with severe right upper quadrant abdominal pain, fever, and jaundice. She reports that the pain started acutely 2 days ago and has been worsening. Her vital signs show a temperature of 38.5°C (101.3°F), heart rate of 110 bpm, blood pressure of 120/80 mmHg, and respiratory rate of 20 breaths/min. Physical examination reveals marked tenderness in the right upper quadrant with guarding and a positive Murphy's sign. Laboratory tests show elevated total bilirubin (4.5 mg/dL, normal <1.2 mg/dL), elevated alkaline phosphatase (350 U/L, normal 40-120 U/L), elevated AST (180 U/L, normal <40 U/L), and elevated GGT (200 U/L, normal <60 U/L). An abdominal ultrasound reveals multiple gallstones within the gallbladder and dilation of the common bile duct (1.2 cm diameter, normal <0.6 cm). Cholecystectomy is performed, and the removed gallbladder is sent for pathological examination. Gross examination shows multiple small, dark, hard stones. Microscopic examination reveals stones composed primarily of calcium bilirubinate pigment, with minimal cholesterol content. Immunohistochemical staining for mucin is negative.

What is the most likely composition of the gallstones found in this patient?

(A) Primarily cholesterol, associated with female sex, obesity, and multiparity.
(B) Primarily calcium bilirubinate pigment, associated with chronic hemolysis and biliary stasis.
(C) Primarily calcium bilirubinate pigment, associated with biliary tract infection and obstruction.
(D) Primarily cholesterol, associated with rapid weight loss and total parenteral nutrition.
(E) Primarily calcium bilirubinate pigment, associated with cirrhosis and portal hypertension.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 62-year-old woman with chronic hemolytic anemia (sickle cell disease). This is a crucial clue pointing towards increased bilirubin production.
- **Symptoms:** Severe RUQ pain, fever, jaundice. This triad suggests acute cholecystitis or choledocholithiasis (common bile duct stones) with possible cholangitis.
- **Physical Exam:** RUQ tenderness, guarding, positive Murphy's sign. Consistent with gallbladder inflammation (cholecystitis).
- **Labs:** Elevated bilirubin (conjugated hyperbilirubinemia likely, given elevated direct fraction implied by elevated GGT and ALP), elevated ALP, AST, GGT. Suggests cholestasis and biliary obstruction/inflammation.
- **Imaging:** Ultrasound shows gallstones and dilated common bile duct. Confirms cholelithiasis and biliary obstruction.
- **Surgery & Pathology:** Cholecystectomy performed. Gross exam shows small, dark, hard stones. Microscopic exam confirms stones composed primarily of calcium bilirubinate pigment with minimal cholesterol. This is the definitive finding.
- **Question:** Asks for the most likely composition of the stones.

The patient's history of chronic hemolytic anemia (sickle cell disease) leads to increased breakdown of red blood cells, resulting in excessive production of unconjugated bilirubin. The liver conjugates this excess bilirubin, but the increased load can overwhelm the system, leading to increased amounts of conjugated bilirubin being excreted into the bile. In the gallbladder, this excess conjugated bilirubin can precipitate with calcium to form calcium bilirubinate pigment stones. These stones are typically small, dark, and hard. The clinical presentation (RUQ pain, fever, jaundice, dilated CBD) is consistent with complications of pigment stones, such as choledocholithiasis and cholangitis.

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
- **Cholesterol Stones:** Form when bile becomes supersaturated with cholesterol, often due to increased cholesterol secretion by the liver or decreased bile salt secretion. Risk factors include the "5 Fs": Female, Forty, Fertile, Fat, Fair. Also associated with rapid weight loss, TPN, and certain medications (e.g., estrogen). They are typically yellow-green, smooth, and often larger.
- **Pigment Stones:** Composed primarily of calcium bilirubinate.
    - **Black Pigment Stones:** Form in sterile bile, often associated with chronic hemolysis (e.g., sickle cell anemia, hereditary spherocytosis) leading to increased unconjugated bilirubin, or cirrhosis (leading to increased conjugated bilirubin). They are typically small, hard, and dark.
    - **Brown Pigment Stones:** Form in infected bile ducts, often associated with biliary stasis and bacterial infection (e.g., E. coli, Klebsiella). Bacteria produce beta-glucuronidase, which deconjugates bilirubin glucuronides, releasing free bilirubin that precipitates with calcium. They are typically soft, greasy, and variable in color.
- **Patient's Case:** The patient has chronic hemolytic anemia (sickle cell disease), a major risk factor for increased bilirubin production. The stones are described as small, dark, hard, and composed primarily of calcium bilirubinate pigment with minimal cholesterol. This morphology and composition are characteristic of black pigment stones associated with chronic hemolysis.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Incorrect. Cholesterol stones are the most common type (80-90%) but are associated with different risk factors (female sex, obesity, multiparity, rapid weight loss, TPN) and typically have a higher cholesterol content. This patient's history of chronic hemolysis strongly points towards pigment stones.
- (C): Incorrect. While biliary tract infection can lead to pigment stones (brown pigment stones), the patient's primary risk factor is chronic hemolysis, and the stones are described as hard and dark, characteristic of black pigment stones, not the soft, greasy brown stones typically found in infection.
- (D): Incorrect. Rapid weight loss and TPN are risk factors for cholesterol stones, not pigment stones. The patient's history of sickle cell disease is the key factor here.
- (E): Incorrect. Cirrhosis is associated with black pigment stones due to increased conjugated bilirubin excretion, but the patient's primary underlying condition is chronic hemolysis (sickle cell disease), which is a more direct and prominent cause in this specific clinical context. While cirrhosis *can* cause pigment stones, the hemolytic anemia is the stronger association given the vignette.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):
| Feature             | Cholesterol Stones                                  | Black Pigment Stones (Calcium Bilirubinate)                 | Brown Pigment Stones (Calcium Bilirubinate)                 |
|----------------------|----------------------------------------------------|-------------------------------------------------------------|-------------------------------------------------------------|
| **Composition**      | Primarily Cholesterol (>50%)                         | Primarily Calcium Bilirubinate (>50%)                       | Primarily Calcium Bilirubinate (>50%)                       |
| **Color**            | Yellow-Green                                        | Black                                                       | Brown/Greenish-Brown                                         |
| **Texture**          | Often smooth, sometimes faceted                     | Hard, brittle                                                | Soft, greasy, moldable                                      |
| **Size**             | Variable, often larger                               | Typically small                                              | Variable                                                    |
| **Associated Factors**| Female, Forty, Fertile, Fat, Fair, Rapid weight loss, TPN | Chronic hemolysis (Sickle cell, spherocytosis), Cirrhosis | Biliary infection, stasis, parasitic infestation (e.g., Clonorchis) |
| **Bile Environment** | Cholesterol supersaturation                          | Sterile bile, increased bilirubin (conjugated or unconjugated) | Infected bile, bacterial beta-glucuronidase activity        |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The key takeaway is to associate specific risk factors and stone characteristics with different types of gallstones. Chronic hemolysis leads to increased bilirubin, predisposing to black pigment stones (calcium bilirubinate). Cholesterol stones are more common overall and linked to metabolic factors. Brown pigment stones are linked to infection. Remember the "5 Fs" for cholesterol stones and hemolysis/cirrhosis for black pigment stones, and infection/stasis for brown pigment stones.
